🚨 突发动态与新闻 / Health News

Published: 2026-10-29  |  Publisher: GOV.UK
A 高优先级公共卫生新闻

国家流感和 COVID-19 监测 报告:2026 年 10 月 29 日(第 44 周) - GOV.UK

National flu and COVID-19 surveillance report: 29 October 2026 (week 44) - GOV.UK
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 29 日(第 44 周)的全国流感和 COVID-19 监测 报告概述了英格兰截至第 43 周的流感、RSV 和 COVID-19 活动。流感活动保持稳定,急诊室 (ED) 因流感样疾病 (ILI) 就诊的人数增加,高于季节性预期水平。RSV 活动增加,而 COVID-19 活动减少,确诊的急性呼吸道感染 (ARI) 病例与前一周相比有所减少。2026-2027 年季节的 疫苗 吸收数据显示,老年人和临床风险群体的吸收率较低,但孕妇的吸收率较高。由于 疫苗接种 记录不完整,前几周丢失了一些数据,但这在第 43 周得到了纠正。报告指出,用于定义活动水平的方法是实验性的,可能会进行修订。总体而言,尽管数据收集存在一些限制,但流感 疫苗接种 计划被认为反映了准确的吸收估计。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该报告涵盖截至2026年第43周(2026年10月19日至10月25日)的数据。
地点与对象
英格兰,提供不同年龄组和人群的流感、RSV 和 COVID-19 活动数据。
事件
该报告详细介绍了 监测 在 2026 年第 43 周在英格兰对流感、RSV 和 COVID-19 的发现,包括急诊室就诊人数、确诊的 ARI 病例和 疫苗 吸收数据。
规模、影响与风险
流感活动稳定并处于低水平,流感样疾病急诊就诊人数增加高于季节性预期水平。RSV 活性增加并处于基线水平。与前一周相比,COVID-19 活动有所减少,急诊室因 COVID-19 类疾病就诊的人数减少,确诊的 ARI 病例也减少。疫苗 摄取数据显示年龄组和风险类别之间存在一些差异。
应对、状态与不确定性
该报告基于截至第 43 周的数据,第 41 周和第 42 周缺少一些 疫苗接种 数据。用于定义活动水平(MEM 和 MSD)的方法被认为是实验性的,可能会进行修订。对于总体 疫苗接种 吸收估计,该数据被认为是准确的。
A high priorityPublic-health news

National flu and COVID-19 surveillance report: 29 October 2026 (week 44) - GOV.UK

News Brief
The National Flu and COVID-19 Surveillance Report for 29 October 2026 (week 44) provides an overview of influenza, RSV, and COVID-19 activity in England up to week 43. Influenza activity remained stable, with increased emergency department (ED) attendances for influenza-like illness (ILI) above seasonally expected levels. RSV activity increased, while COVID-19 activity decreased, with fewer confirmed acute respiratory infection (ARI) cases compared to the previous week. Vaccine uptake data for the 2026-2027 season showed lower rates among older adults and those in clinical risk groups, but higher uptake among pregnant women. Some data was missing in earlier weeks due to incomplete vaccination records, but this was corrected in week 43. The report notes that the methods used to define activity levels are experimental and may be revised. Overall, the influenza vaccination programme is considered to reflect accurate uptake estimates despite some limitations in data collection.
Original source text (English)
This publication is licensed under the terms of the Open Government Licence v3.0 except where otherwise stated. To view this licence, visit nationalarchives.gov.uk/doc/open-government-licence/version/3 or write to the Information Policy Team, The National Archives, Kew, London TW9 4DU, or email: psi@nationalarchives.gov.uk . Where we have identified any third party copyright information you will need to obtain permission from the copyright holders concerned. This publication is available at https://www.gov.uk/government/statistics/national-flu-and-covid-19-surveillance-reports-2026-to-2027-season/national-flu-and-covid-19-surveillance-report-29-october-2026-week-44 This report summarises the information from the surveillance systems which are used to monitor COVID-19 (caused by severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2)), influenza, respiratory syncytial virus ( RSV ) and diseases caused by other seasonal respiratory viruses in England. This report is based on data up to and including week 43 of 2026 (19 October and 25 October 2026). The main messages of this report are: Annual reports for the 2025 to 2026 season: Influenza in the UK, annual epidemiological report: winter 2025 to 2026 (published 28 May 2026) Epidemiology of COVID-19 in England (published 25 June 2026) Seasonal influenza vaccine uptake in all GP patients: annual report 2025 to 2026 (published 28 May 2026) Seasonal influenza vaccine uptake in school-aged children: annual report 2025 to 2026 (published 28 May 2026) Seasonal influenza vaccine uptake in frontline healthcare workers: annual report 2025 to 2026 (published 28 May 2026) Influenza activity was stable across most indicators and is circulating at low levels. Emergency department ( ED ) attendances for influenza-like-illness ( ILI ) increased and were above seasonally expected levels. The number of influenza-confirmed acute respiratory infection ( ARI ) incidents increased compared with the previous week. Weekly influenza vaccine uptake for the 2026 to 2027 season is reported for the fourth time this season. Data is for week 43 (up to 25 October 2026). Influenza vaccination data was unavailable or incomplete at the time of analysis in weeks 41 and 42, and led to several cohorts not being published in week 42. This has been corrected for this week’s report (week 43), and all cohorts are included. This will be corrected retrospectively in the report for week 42. There remains an issue that affects the attribution of vaccinations by provider setting. This is not expected to affect overall vaccination uptake estimates, which are considered to remain an accurate reflection of the influenza vaccination programme. Compared with the equivalent week last season (week 43 data, 2025 to 2026), vaccine uptake is lower among patients aged 65 years and over (53.6% vs 56.9%), patients aged 6 months to under 65 years in clinical risk groups (24.8% vs 26.2%), those aged 2 years (29.7% vs 30.8%), and those aged 3 years (29.5% vs 30.7%). Vaccine uptake is higher among pregnant women (29.1% vs 27.8%). COVID-19 activity decreased and is circulating at low levels. ED attendances for COVID-19-like illness decreased. The number of COVID-19 confirmed acute respiratory infections ( ARI ) incidents decreased compared with the previous week. By the end of week 43 2026, 42.5% of all people aged 75 years and over in England had been vaccinated with an autumn 2026 dose. Similarly, 18.2% of all people aged under 75 years with a weakened immune system in England had been vaccinated with an autumn 2026 dose since 1 October 2026. RSV activity increased and is circulating at baseline levels. ED attendances for acute bronchiolitis were stable and below seasonally expected levels. Note 1: these indicators use the moving epidemic method ( MEM ) and the mean standard deviation method ( MSD ) to define thresholds to determine their respective levels of activity. Further information on these methods can be found in Influenza surveillance in Europe: establishing epidemic thresholds by the Moving Epidemic Method and Setting thresholds to determine COVID-19 activity levels using the mean standard deviation ( MSD ) method, England, 2022 to 2024 . The MEM approach is well-established for some influenza surveillance indicators, however, for other indicators both the MEM and MSD are experimental and may be subject to future revision. Influenza laboratory surveillance and GP swabbing positivity have transitioned from using MEM to using MSD . These approaches will be considered alongside expert opinion and triangulation of other data sources. The Second Generation Surveillance System ( SGSS ) captures test result information for notifiable infectious diseases, including COVID-19 and influenza, from laboratories in England. The unified sample dataset ( USD ) stores all SARS-CoV-2 test results reported to SGSS , Respiratory DataMart and UKHSA laboratories, and is used to calculate the percentage of tests positive for SARS-CoV-2 among all SARS-CoV-2 tests conducted. As of 27 October 2026, there were a total of 1,847 COVID-19 cases identified in hospital settings in week 43, decreasing from 2,151 cases in the previous week. SARS-CoV-2 (COVID-19) PCR positivity in hospital settings decreased slightly in week 43, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 8.3% up to Sunday 25 October 2026 compared with 8.8% for the same period on the previous Sunday. Positivity rates were highest among those aged 85 years and over with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 11.1% up to Sunday 25 October 2026. This has decreased compared to 13.0% in the same age group on the previous Sunday. Note 2: data from previous seasons is aligned by day. Note 3: testing policy and practice may change over time which can impact positivity rates, therefore comparisons over time should be interpreted with caution. Notable changes in testing policy occurred during 2022 to 2023, which are outlined in the data quality report . Note 4: the highlighted line corresponds to the age group in the subplot title, grey lines correspond to all other age groups. Influenza positivity remained stable in week 43, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 6.6% up to Sunday 25 October 2026. This is compared with 6.4% on the previous Sunday. By type, influenza A positivity increased slightly in week 43, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 5.8% up to Sunday 25 October 2026. Influenza B positivity increased in the same week, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 0.3% up to Sunday 25 October 2026. Influenza A positivity rates were highest among those aged between 5 and 11 years, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 16.6% up to Sunday 25 October 2026. This has increased from 11.3% in the same age group on the previous Sunday. Influenza B positivity rates for the same period were highest among those aged between 5 and 11 years, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 1.8%. This has decreased from 2.3% in the same age group on the previous Sunday. RSV swab-positivity based PCR test results reported through SGSS have been included as a pilot indicator from week 49 2025 onwards. RSV positivity increased in week 43, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 1.6% up to Sunday 25 October 2026, compared with 1.1% up to the previous Sunday. RSV positivity rates were highest in those aged between 0 and 4 years with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 10.1% up to Sunday 25 October 2026 in week 43. This has increased from 7.8% in the same age group on the previous Sunday. Respiratory DataMart is a sentinel laboratory-based surveillance system where participating laboratories report positive and negative test results for a number of respiratory viruses from samples primarily taken in hospital. A small proportion of primary care samples are also included in this reporting. In week 43, data is based on reporting from 9 out of the 14 sentinel laboratories. In week 43, 4,070 respiratory specimens reported through the Respiratory DataMart System were tested for influenza. There were 258 positive samples for influenza: 179 influenza A (not subtyped), 11 influenza A (H3N2), 51 influenza A (H1N1)pdm09, and 19 influenza B. Overall, influenza positivity increased slightly to 6.3% in week 43 compared with 5.9% in the previous week. In week 43, 4,952 respiratory specimens reported through the Respiratory DataMart System were tested for SARS-CoV-2. There were 376 positive samples for SARS-CoV-2. SARS-CoV-2 positivity remained stable at 7.6% compared with 7.3% in the previous week, with the highest positivity in those aged between 65 and 79 years at 10.0%. RSV positivity increased to 1.5%, with the highest positivity in those aged under 5 years at 8.3%. Adenovirus positivity increased to 1.5%, with the highest positivity in those aged under 5 years at 5.1%. Human metapneumovirus ( hMPV ) positivity decreased to 0.1%, with the highest positivity in those aged between 15 and 44 years at 0.4%. Parainfluenza positivity decreased slightly to 2.9%, with the highest positivity in those aged between 5 and 14 years at 11.3%. Rhinovirus positivity decreased slightly to 16.5%, with the highest positivity in those aged under 5 years at 29.1%. DataMart data is provisional and subject to retrospective updates. Note 5: shading represents 95% confidence intervals. This section is updated fortnightly. Data below was last updated in the week 43 report. UKHSA conducts genomic surveillance of SARS-CoV-2 lineages. This section provides an overview of circulating lineages in England, derived from data on sequenced PCR -positive SARS-CoV-2 samples in SGSS . The prevalence of UKHSA -designated lineages among sequenced cases is presented in Figure 13. To account for reporting delays, we report the proportion of lineages within COVID-19 cases that have had a sequenced positive sample between 14 September 2026 and 27 September 2026. Note that low sequencing numbers, especially within the latest reporting period, will impact the accuracy of the prevalence estimates. These most recent figures should therefore be interpreted with caution. Note that lineages will be grouped independently from their parent lineage once they reach sufficient prevalence, and may be re-grouped into their parent lineage if their prevalence subsequently falls. The data quality report contains more information on lineage groupings. Analysis of viruses from primary and secondary care shows that circulating A(H1N1)pdm09 viruses show low genetic diversity and all belong to clade D.3.1.1. Observations published from the WHO influenza vaccine composition meeting (VCM) in September 2026 suggest low reactivity of some viruses within the D.3.1.1 subclade with post-infection ferret antisera raised against A/Missouri/11/2025 (H1N1)pdm09-like virus – the A(H1N1)pdm09 component in 2026 Southern and 2026/2027 Northern Hemisphere cell- and egg-based influenza vaccines - and human sera post-vaccination with the Southern Hemisphere vaccine 2026. It is not yet clear how vaccine effectiveness against clinical disease may be affected by these observations. Vaccines typically still provide protection against severe clinical disease when drifted strains are seen with only a modest reduction in effectiveness. The characterisation of circulating A(H3N2) viruses shows that A(H3N2) viruses are currently showing low genetic diversity. All sequenced viruses to date belong to genetic subclade K. To date, only a small number of influenza B viruses have been detected, which show some genetic diversity. Between week 35 2026 (week ending 30 August 2026) and week 43 2026 (week ending 25 October 2026), the UKHSA respiratory virus unit ( RVU ) has genetically characterised 297 seasonal influenza viruses, and identified 184 influenza A(H1N1)pdm09 viruses, 86 influenza A(H3N2) viruses, and 27 influenza B viruses. Details of the characterised viruses by subtype are shown in Tables 1, 2 and 3. The RVU has confirmed by genome sequencing the detection of live attenuated influenza vaccine ( LAIV ) viruses in 11 influenza A/B positive samples, collected from children aged between 2 and 16 years. UKHSA RVU performs antigenic characterisation of influenza A(H1N1)pdm09, influenza A(H3N2) and influenza B viruses using haemagglutination inhibition ( HI ) assays. Data from these assays are used to compare how similar the currently circulating influenza viruses are to the strains included in seasonal influenza vaccines, and to monitor for changes in circulating influenza viruses. Similarity of currently circulating influenza strains to vaccine strains is defined as having an antibody titre within 4-fold when compared with reference viruses representative of the vaccine strain. Antigenic characterisation of viruses in the current reporting period is underway. A(H1N1)pdm09: 5 A(H1N1)pdm09 viruses have been antigenically characterised and 3 were similar to reference viruses representative of the A/Missouri/11/2025 (H1N1)pdm09-like Northern Hemisphere 2026/27 (H1N1)pdm09 vaccine strains. A(H3N2): 6 A(H3N2) viruses have been antigenically characterised and 6 were similar to reference viruses representative of the A/Darwin/1454/2025 (H3N2)-like and A/Darwin/1415/2025 (H3N2) like Northern Hemisphere 2026/27 (H3N2) vaccine strains. Influenza positive samples are screened for mutations in the virus neuraminidase ( NA ) and the cap-dependent endonuclease of the polymerase acidic protein (PA) genes known to confer neuraminidase inhibitor (oseltamivir and zanamivir) or baloxavir resistance, respectively. Results from this surveillance are given in table 4. There have been five detections with mutations related to reduced susceptibility/resistance to oseltamivir and/or reduced susceptibility to baloxavir. Data is presented on viral ARI incidents in different settings that are reported to UKHSA health protection teams ( HPTs ). Reporting practices are known to vary between seasons and between regions. Any interpretation of temporal and regional trends should consider the likelihood of differences in reporting of ARI incidents over time and between regions. There were 112 new ARI incidents reported in week 43 in England. These included: 101 incidents from care homes, of which 24 were due to SARS-CoV-2, 17 were due to influenza A, 4 were due to other pathogens, 3 were due to influenza (no type information available) and 3 were due to multiple pathogens. No pathogen was reported in 50 incidents 4 incidents from hospitals, of which 3 were due to influenza A and 1 was due to SARS-CoV-2 4 incidents from educational settings, of which 2 were due to influenza (no type information available) and 1 was due to other pathogens. No pathogen was reported in 1 incident 1 incident from prisons, of which 1 was due to SARS-CoV-2 2 incidents from other settings, of which 1 was due to SARS-CoV-2 and 1 was due to influenza (no type information available) Syndromic surveillance collects data from various healthcare sources where presentations are classified by patterns of symptoms compatible with specific infections. Time
The report covers data up to and including week 43 of 2026 (19 October and 25 October 2026).
Location and population
England, with data on influenza, RSV, and COVID-19 activity across different age groups and populations.
Event
The report details the surveillance findings for influenza, RSV, and COVID-19 in England during week 43 of 2026, including ED attendances, confirmed ARI cases, and vaccine uptake data.
Scale, impact and risk
Influenza activity was stable and circulating at low levels, with increased ED attendances for ILI above seasonally expected levels. RSV activity increased and was at baseline levels. COVID-19 activity decreased, with lower ED attendances for COVID-19-like illness and fewer confirmed ARI cases compared to the previous week. Vaccine uptake data showed some variations among age groups and risk categories.
Response, status and uncertainty
The report is based on data up to week 43, with some vaccination data missing in weeks 41 and 42. The methods used for defining activity levels (MEM and MSD) are noted as experimental and may be subject to revision. The data is considered accurate for overall vaccination uptake estimates.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

季节性流感 会增加心脏病发作和中风的风险。- 越南.vn

Seasonal influenza increases the risk of heart attack and stroke. - Vietnam.vn
新闻简报Vietnam.vn 发表于 2026 年 10 月 29 日的文章强调,季节性流感 会增加心脏病发作和中风的风险,尤其是老年人和患有心血管疾病或慢性疾病的人。它解释说,该病毒可以引发全身炎症,导致血液凝固、血管功能和器官供氧发生变化。研究表明,感染后的某些时期,心肌梗塞的风险可能增加6-10倍,中风的风险可能增加3-10倍。这些数字代表相对风险增加,而不是普遍风险。即使没有心脏病史的人也可能发生心血管并发症。专家强调了年度流感 疫苗接种 的重要性,在适当的条件下,它可以提供高达 90% 的保护,但请注意,其功效因流行毒株、年龄和健康状况而异。还建议采取个人卫生措施,例如经常洗手和戴口罩,以降低感染风险。该文章强调,除了典型的流感症状外,还需要积极预防和监测。
查看新闻五要素
时间
文章发表于2026年10月29日。据指出,即使在疾病早期,季节性流感 引起的心血管并发症的风险也会增加。
地点与对象
这篇文章来自越南。它提到老年人、患有心血管疾病的人和患有慢性疾病的人是特别脆弱的人群。
事件
据报道,季节性流感 会增加心脏病发作和中风的风险,特别是对于已有血管损伤或慢性疾病的个体。这得到了心脏病专家的证实并得到了研究的支持。
规模、影响与风险
研究表明,感染流感后的某些时期,心肌梗塞的风险可能增加约6-10倍,而中风的风险可能增加约3-10倍。这些数字反映了相对风险的增加,而不是所有患者都面临更高的风险。即使没有心脏病史的个体也可能发生心血管事件。
应对、状态与不确定性
专家强调预防、早期发现和年度流感疫苗接种的重要性。根据年龄、健康状况、专业指导推荐疫苗接种。然而,疫苗 的保护功效根据接种个体的循环病毒株、年龄和健康状况而有所不同。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Seasonal influenza increases the risk of heart attack and stroke. - Vietnam.vn

News Brief
The article from Vietnam.vn, published on 2026-10-29, highlights that seasonal influenza increases the risk of heart attack and stroke, particularly in the elderly and individuals with pre-existing cardiovascular conditions or chronic illnesses. It explains that the virus can trigger systemic inflammation, leading to changes in blood clotting, vascular function, and oxygen supply to organs. Studies indicate that the risk of myocardial infarction may rise by 6-10 times, and stroke risk by 3-10 times during certain periods after infection. These figures represent relative risk increases, not universal risks. Cardiovascular complications can occur even in those without prior heart disease. Experts stress the importance of annual flu vaccination, which can offer up to 90% protection under suitable conditions, but note that its efficacy varies based on the circulating strain, age, and health status. Personal hygiene measures, such as frequent handwashing and mask-wearing, are also recommended to reduce infection risk. The article underscores the need for proactive prevention and monitoring beyond typical flu symptoms.
Original source text (English)
English Login Homepage Topic News Political System Destination Event Travel Happy Vietnam Enterprise Product Heritage Museum Figure Multimedia Data Home Culture – Society Y tế Seasonal influenza increases the risk of heart attack and stroke. Seasonal influenza can cause serious complications beyond the respiratory tract, including myocardial infarction, stroke, and heart failure. The risk is particularly noteworthy in the elderly, those with cardiovascular disease, or those with chronic illnesses. Báo Đầu tư•29/10/2026 Follow Vietnam.vn on Google Add Vietnam.vn on Google 0 Cardiologists warn that influenza infection can be a contributing factor to heart attacks and strokes, especially in individuals with pre-existing vascular damage or poorly controlled underlying conditions. Worryingly, the risk of complications can increase even in the early days of illness, requiring close monitoring of the patient's health. Seasonal influenza can cause serious complications beyond the respiratory tract, including myocardial infarction, stroke, and heart failure. The risk is particularly noteworthy in the elderly, those with cardiovascular disease, or those with chronic illnesses. Associate Professor Tran Song Giang, Deputy Director of the Cardiovascular Institute at Bach Mai Hospital, said that influenza virus infection can trigger a systemic inflammatory response, causing changes in blood clotting, vascular function, and the ability to supply oxygen to organs. In individuals with pre-existing atherosclerosis, the inflammatory response can make atherosclerotic plaques less stable, increasing the risk of rupture of the outer layer. This process can trigger the formation of blood clots, causing blockages in the flow of blood within the artery. If a blood clot blocks a coronary artery, the patient is at risk of a heart attack. If the blockage occurs in a blood vessel supplying the brain, the consequence can be an ischemic stroke. This mechanism explains why a respiratory infection can become a trigger for dangerous circulatory events, especially in the elderly or patients with pre-existing vascular damage. Khám phá thêm Tìm hiểu lịch sử Đọc Mẹo Du Lịch Khám phá ĐNA Beyond just causing inflammation, influenza also increases the workload on the heart. During infection, body temperature rises, and the heart rate often increases, leading to increased oxygen consumption by the heart muscle. Associate Professor Tran Song Giang also noted that in some cases, the influenza virus can be associated with myocardial damage, causing myocarditis and affecting the heart's ability to contract. Of particular concern is that cardiovascular events don't only occur in people already diagnosed with heart disease. Individuals with no prior history of cardiovascular disease can still experience complications during influenza infection, although the risk is influenced by age, health status, and pre-existing risk factors. Studies presented by Associate Professor Dr. Tran Song Giang show a notable correlation between influenza infection and acute cardiovascular events. Several studies have noted that the risk of myocardial infarction may increase by approximately 6-10 times, while the risk of stroke increases by approximately 3-10 times during certain periods following influenza infection. These figures reflect the relative risk increase observed in specific studies and do not mean that all patients with influenza are at a higher risk of heart attack or stroke. You may also like Nevertheless, research shows that influenza should not be taken lightly, especially for those with pre-existing cardiovascular risk factors. Proactive prevention and early detection of abnormal symptoms are crucial in limiting complications. The elderly are a group that requires special attention due to their weakened immune response, while the incidence of chronic diseases is often higher than in younger people. Given the risks associated with influenza infection, experts emphasize the importance of prevention, especially for the elderly and those with underlying health conditions. Annual flu vaccination is one of the important measures to reduce the risk of contracting the flu and the serious complications it causes. Vaccination should be administered according to age, health status, and professional guidance. However, vaccination does not completely eliminate the possibility of contracting the flu or experiencing complications. People still need to maintain health protection measures, especially during periods when the flu virus is circulating widely. Along with vaccination, personal hygiene measures such as frequent handwashing, covering your mouth when coughing or sneezing, wearing a mask when experiencing respiratory symptoms, keeping living spaces well-ventilated, and limiting close contact with sick people remain important in reducing the risk of infection. Experts note that cardiovascular complications related to influenza can appear even during the acute phase of the illness, so monitoring should not focus solely on fever, cough, or sore throat. According to Dr. Nguyen Van My of the Long Chau Vaccination System, the protective efficacy of the flu vaccine under suitable conditions can reach approximately 90%, but the actual level of protection varies depending on the circulating virus strain, age, and health status of the vaccinated individual. The protective ability can also decrease over time, while the flu virus is constantly mutating and new circulating strains are emerging. Therefore, annual flu vaccination is crucial in maintaining protection, especially for young children, the elderly, pregnant women, people with chronic diseases, and those with weakened immune systems. According to Doctor Nguyen Van My, the body usually needs about two weeks after vaccination to develop a protective immune response. Therefore, people should proactively get vaccinated before the flu season or as soon as conditions permit, instead of waiting until the number of cases increases before getting vaccinated. You may also like According to experts, vaccines do not guarantee absolute prevention of all cases of influenza, but they play an important role in reducing the risk of illness and limiting serious consequences. Complete and timely vaccination, including annual booster shots, can contribute to reducing the risk of pneumonia, respiratory failure, hospitalization, and death from influenza. Besides vaccination, the Ministry of Health recommends that people wear masks correctly in healthcare facilities, on public transport, in crowded places, or in enclosed spaces with poor ventilation. People with respiratory symptoms should limit close contact with others and proactively take time off from school or work to avoid infection. Washing hands frequently with soap, covering your mouth and nose when coughing or sneezing, regularly cleaning frequently touched surfaces, and keeping your living space well-ventilated also help reduce the risk of virus transmission. For children with the flu, parents should pay attention to symptoms such as difficulty breathing, rapid breathing, cyanosis, lethargy, seizures, signs of dehydration, or a worsening of the condition after a period of apparent improvement. These are signs that require prompt medical attention and treatment; self-treatment at home should not be prolonged. The Department of Disease Prevention also advises people not to self-medicate with antiviral or antibiotic drugs without a prescription from a healthcare professional. Individuals in high-risk groups should contact a healthcare facility early if they experience suspicious symptoms to receive appropriate advice, assessment, and treatment. Location and population
The article is from Vietnam. It mentions the elderly, individuals with cardiovascular disease, and those with chronic illnesses as particularly vulnerable populations.
Event
Seasonal influenza is reported to increase the risk of heart attack and stroke, especially in individuals with pre-existing vascular damage or chronic illnesses. This is confirmed by cardiologists and supported by studies.
Scale, impact and risk

These figures reflect relative risk increases, not that all patients are at higher risk. Cardiovascular events can occur even in individuals without prior heart disease.

季节性流感可导致儿童严重并发症;家长应积极采取预防措施。- 越南.vn

Seasonal influenza can cause serious complications in children; parents should proactively take preventive measures. - Vietnam.vn
新闻简报Vietnam.vn发表于2026-10-28的文章强调了季节性流感对儿童的风险,强调其可导致急性肺水肿、暴发性心肌炎、心源性休克等严重并发症。一名7岁儿童被送往第一儿童医院,情况危急,症状包括高烧、呼吸困难、发绀等。该医院报告称,10 月初平均每天进行 2,300 多次呼吸道检查,其中 季节性流感 是主要原因。儿童医院 1 的医生指出,即使是健康的儿童,这种疾病也会迅速进展,并建议家长如果症状恶化应立即就医。文章还讨论了每年预防流感的重要性,包括最近推出了针对 2 岁至 18 岁以下儿童的阿斯利康鼻喷雾剂 疫苗。这款针对 A/H1N1、A/H3N2 和 B/Victoria 毒株的 疫苗 已在 34 个国家使用,并经过了 20 多年的安全监测。文章强调,家长需要识别严重疾病的早期迹象,避免给孩子自行服用 抗病毒药物 或抗生素药物。
查看新闻五要素
时间
文章发表于2026年10月28日。事件日期没有明确说明,但一名 7 岁儿童因甲型流感而病情危重的病例被提及发生在 10 月初。
地点与对象
该事件报告发生于越南,具体提及胡志明市儿童医院1号和儿童医院2号。受影响人群包括儿童,尤其是学校和托儿所的儿童及其家庭。
事件
一名7岁儿童因流感A病毒引发急性肺水肿、暴发性心肌炎和心源性休克被紧急送往儿童医院1号治疗。医院还报告称,在10月初每天平均进行超过2,300例呼吸道检查。季节性流感被强调为导致儿童严重并发症的主要原因,包括肺炎、呼吸衰竭、脑炎和心肌炎。
规模、影响与风险
季节性流感每年估计在全球范围内导致10亿例病例,其中3-5百万例为严重病例。在儿童医院1号,呼吸道疾病占总检查量的约70%,其中季节性流感是主要致病因素。文章强调了严重并发症的风险,并强调需要采取积极的预防措施。
应对、状态与不确定性
医院使用ECMO、持续血液透析、低温治疗以及针对过度炎症的治疗来控制孩子的病情。近 12 天后,孩子脱离了 ECMO 和机械通气。文章还提到针对2岁至18岁以下儿童推出的鼻喷雾流感疫苗,为注射剂疫苗提供了替代方案。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Seasonal influenza can cause serious complications in children; parents should proactively take preventive measures. - Vietnam.vn

News Brief
The article from Vietnam.vn, published on 2026-10-28, highlights the risks of seasonal influenza in children, emphasizing that it can lead to severe complications such as acute pulmonary edema, fulminant myocarditis, and cardiogenic shock. A 7-year-old child was admitted to Children's Hospital 1 in critical condition, with symptoms including high fever, difficulty breathing, and cyanosis. The hospital reported an average of over 2,300 respiratory examinations per day in early October, with seasonal influenza being a primary cause. Doctors at Children's Hospital 1 noted that the disease can progress rapidly, even in healthy children, and advised parents to seek immediate medical attention if symptoms worsen. The article also discusses the importance of annual flu prevention, including the recent launch of the AstraZeneca nasal spray vaccine for children aged 2 to under 18. This vaccine, which targets A/H1N1, A/H3N2, and B/Victoria strains, has been used in 34 countries and has undergone over 20 years of safety monitoring. The article underscores the need for parents to recognize early signs of severe illness and avoid self-medicating children with antiviral or antibiotic drugs.
Original source text (English)
English Login Homepage Topic News Political System Destination Event Travel Happy Vietnam Enterprise Product Heritage Museum Figure Multimedia Data Home Culture – Society Y tế Seasonal influenza can cause serious complications in children; parents should proactively take preventive measures. Seasonal influenza can cause serious complications, even death, in otherwise healthy children. Doctors advise parents to recognize early signs of worsening symptoms and proactively take annual flu precautions for their children. Báo Tin Tức•28/10/2026 Follow Vietnam.vn on Google Add Vietnam.vn on Google 0 From fever and cough to cardiogenic shock and severe respiratory failure. Seasonal influenza is easily spread in schools, daycare centers, and other communal living environments. According to the World Health Organization (WHO), the world records an estimated one billion cases of seasonal influenza each year, including 3-5 million severe cases. A 7-year-old child in critical condition due to influenza A causing acute pulmonary edema, fulminant myocarditis, and cardiogenic shock is being treated at Children's Hospital 1. Photo: Provided by the hospital. In early October, Children's Hospital 2 ( Ho Chi Minh City) recorded an average of over 2,300 respiratory examinations per day. At Children's Hospital 1, respiratory illnesses accounted for approximately 70% of total examinations, with the main causative agents including seasonal influenza, adenovirus, and respiratory syncytial virus (RSV). Khám phá thêm Tìm hiểu lịch sử Đọc Mẹo Du Lịch Khám phá ĐNA Associate Professor Dr. Pham Van Quang, Head of the Intensive Care and Toxicology Department at Children's Hospital 1, said that seasonal influenza is usually caused by influenza A and B viruses. The disease starts suddenly with fever, chills, headache, muscle aches, fatigue, dry cough, sore throat, nasal congestion or runny nose. Children may also experience nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea. Most patients experience fever for 3-5 days and then recover, while the cough may last longer. However, some cases can progress severely, causing pneumonia, myocarditis, cardiovascular collapse, liver and kidney damage, and be life-threatening. The hospital once admitted a 7-year-old child in critical condition due to influenza A causing acute pulmonary edema, fulminant myocarditis, and cardiogenic shock. The day before admission, the child had a dry cough, runny nose, and persistent high fever that responded poorly to antipyretics. The following morning, the child continued to have a fever, felt weak, and had difficulty breathing. The family took the child to Vung Tau General Hospital and then transferred him to Children's Hospital 1. Upon admission, the child was cyanotic, in cardiovascular collapse, and suffering from severe respiratory failure. Doctors had to use ECMO, an extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (ECMO) system, combined with continuous hemodialysis, hypothermia to protect the brain, and treatment for excessive inflammation. After nearly 12 days, the child was weaned off ECMO and mechanical ventilation; respiratory, cardiovascular, and other organ functions gradually recovered. According to Dr. Truong Huu Khanh, Vice President of the Ho Chi Minh City Infectious Diseases Association, seasonal influenza can cause serious complications even in healthy children. Besides pneumonia and respiratory failure, children may develop encephalitis, altered consciousness, coma, myocarditis, or bacterial superinfection leading to sepsis, shock, and multiple organ failure. You may also like Some children who survive severe complications may suffer from lasting effects on motor skills, memory, and cognition. Meanwhile, initial symptoms such as fever, cough, runny nose, and fatigue are easily mistaken for a common cold, causing parents to be slow to recognize when the illness has progressed to a serious stage. Dr. Quang noted that children should be taken to the hospital immediately if they experience difficulty breathing, rapid breathing, labored breathing, chest pain, cyanosis, altered consciousness, or if they have cold hands and feet, mottled skin, a rapid and weak pulse. Parents should not self-medicate their children with antiviral or antibiotic drugs due to the risk of side effects and drug resistance. More flu prevention options for children According to Dr. Truong Huu Khanh, many countries have included annual flu vaccination in their public health protection strategies. Children with the flu can transmit the virus within families and schools, increasing the risk of infection for the elderly and those with underlying health conditions. Therefore, flu prevention for children also helps reduce the risk of infection for family members. On October 28th, VNVC Vaccination System launched the AstraZeneca (UK) nasal spray flu vaccine, becoming the first vaccination center in Vietnam to offer this vaccine. Photo: VNVC Dr. Bach Thi Chinh, Medical Director of the VNVC Vaccination System, said that children often have close contact with friends at school and daycare, making them susceptible to illness and bringing viruses home. Preventive measures need to be maintained annually. In addition to injectable vaccines, on October 28th, VNVC launched the AstraZeneca (UK) nasal spray flu vaccine for children aged 2 to under 18 years old. The vaccine is delivered directly into the nasal mucosa, providing an alternative option for children who are afraid of needles. According to Dr. Chinh, this is a live attenuated vaccine that protects against three strains of the virus: A/H1N1, A/H3N2, and B/Victoria. The vaccine has undergone more than 20 years of safety monitoring and has been deployed in 34 countries and territories, with over 200 million doses distributed. The vaccine stimulates an immune response in the nasal and throat mucosa, where influenza viruses typically enter; it generates antibodies through systemic immunity and stimulates cellular immunity, helping to identify and eliminate virus-infected cells. According to research data presented by the organization, the vaccine's effectiveness in preventing influenza reaches 88% against virus strains similar to those in the vaccine. AstraZeneca experts note that, although needle-free, spray vaccines must still be administered by healthcare professionals or under professional supervision. Children need to be screened, appropriately prescribed, and vaccination safety procedures must be followed. You may also like Spray vaccines need to be stored at very low temperatures, between -20 and -30 degrees Celsius. Photo: VVNC According to information from VNVC, this vaccine is stored at a temperature of -20 to -30 degrees Celsius. Before use, the product is thawed according to the procedure, then stored at 2-8 degrees Celsius for a maximum of 15 weeks as instructed by the manufacturer. Proper storage requires specialized cold storage, continuous temperature monitoring systems, and shelf life control. These requirements must be maintained throughout transportation, distribution, and use to ensure vaccine quality. Dr. Khanh noted that the nasal spray vaccine is an additional preventative option, making it easier for parents to protect their children from the flu. "Regardless of the method used, proactively preventing the flu annually remains essential to reduce the risk of illness and related complications," the doctor emphasized. 针对 季节性流感 的 疫苗接种 活动将于周一开始 - TVMnews.mt

Vaccination campaign against seasonal influenza starts on Monday - TVMnews.mt
新闻简报马耳他政府于周一(2026-11-01)启动了针对季节性流感、新冠状病毒(COVID-19)和肺炎球菌病的疫苗接种接种活动。该计划为符合条件的个人提供免费的疫苗接种,包括六月龄以上儿童以及易感人群,如老年人、六月龄至五岁儿童、孕妇和患有慢性疾病的人群。副首相是首批接种疫苗接种的人之一,鼓励公众效仿。疫苗接种将在马耳他和戈佐的卫生中心、国家免疫诊所以及参与的市镇理事会提供,同时也会在获得政府购买疫苗的私人医生处提供。此外,还组织了针对婴儿、儿童和青少年的特别接种会,无需预约。该活动旨在降低呼吸道感染导致严重疾病和并发症的风险,特别是在冬季。尽管目前尚未报告医院入院人数增加,但卫生当局强调应在冬季来临前接种疫苗接种以确保防护。公众被建议联系当地市镇理事会以获取接种会详情,并访问流感.gov.mt获取更多信息。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Vaccination campaign against seasonal influenza starts on Monday - TVMnews.mt

News Brief
The Maltese government has launched a vaccination campaign against seasonal influenza, COVID-19, and pneumococcal disease, starting on Monday, 2026-11-01. The initiative offers free vaccines to eligible individuals, including those over six months of age and vulnerable groups such as the elderly, children aged six months to five years, pregnant women, and people with chronic medical conditions. The Deputy Prime Minister, who was among the first to be vaccinated, encouraged the public to follow suit. Vaccinations will be available at Health Centres in Malta and Gozo, National Immunisation clinics, and participating Local Councils, as well as private doctors who have obtained government-purchased vaccines. Special sessions for children, adolescents, and pregnant women are also being organized, with no appointments required. The campaign aims to reduce the risk of serious illness and complications from respiratory infections, particularly during the winter months. While no increase in hospital admissions has been reported yet, health authorities emphasize the importance of vaccination before the winter season to ensure protection. The public is advised to contact their Local Council for details on sessions and to visit influenza.gov.mt for more information.
Original source text (English)
EN MT News Current Affairs Foreign Affairs European Union Sport TVMi LIVE TVM TVM+ TVMSport+ Magic Radju Malta Radju Malta 2 JOBS Current Affairs Vaccination campaign against seasonal influenza starts on Monday by TVM Newsroom October 30, 2026 October 30, 2026 Aqra bil- Malti The campaign will offer free vaccinations against seasonal influenza, COVID-19, and pneumococcal disease to eligible persons. Vaccination remains one of the most effective ways to reduce the risk of serious illness and complications from respiratory infections, particularly during the winter months. The Deputy Prime Minister, who was one of the first to be vaccinated, urged the Maltese and Gozitans to follow suit. Dr Borg thanked all staff at the Ministry and health professionals, who worked for the vaccines to be available on time despite international challenges, and for the vaccination service to be provided in the best possible way. “The Health Ministry will start the seasonal vaccination on Monday.I urge all those who normally get vaccinated to do so again, as this is important for them and for those around them. I also urge those considering getting vaccinated for the first time to get informed, make the decision and come forward to be vaccinated,” Sr Borg said. This year, the service will be available at Health Centres in Malta and Gozo, National Immunisation clinics for children, adolescents and pregnant women, and participating Local Councils, as well as doctors in the private sector who obtain vaccines purchased by the Government. The public is urged to make use of the service most convenient or them. The Government is offering these vaccines at no charge as follows: Vaccine against seasonal influenza, available to persons over the age of six months; Vaccine against COVID-19, available to persons over the age of six months; PCV20 against pneumococcus, for 60+ adults as well as adults under the age of 60 suffering from medical conditions that make them eligible. The PCV20 vaccine is only administered once, meaning that those who have already taken it need not take it again. The three vaccines can be taken on the same day, according to the clinical availability of the person and the evaluation of the administering health professional. From 2 November, adults and adolescents from age 13+ can attend at Health Centre treatment rooms and no appointment is needed for the times and places indicated below. Between Sunday and Friday, including public holidays but excluding Saturdays, vaccinations will be administered between 1.00 p.m. and 7.00 p.m. at these centres: Cospicua Health Centre; Birkirkara Health Centre; Ħal Qormi Health Centre; Rabat, Malta Heakth Centre.. From Monday to Sunday, including Saturdays, Sundays and public holidays, vaccinations will be adminiwtered between 1.00 p.m. and 7.00 p.m. at these centres: Mosta Health Centre; Paola Health Centre; Floriana Health Centre; Gżira Health Centre; ; Ħal Kirkop Health Centre; Rabat, Gozo Health Centre. All those attending should present a valid Identity Card. Vaccination against pneumococcus is available regularly for 60+ adults. Vulnerable adults under the age of 60 need a medical certificate confirming their eligibility for this vaccination. There will be special sessions for vaccination of babies, children and adolescents, aged between six months and 12 years, as well as for pregnant women, at these clinics: For Malta: National Immunisation Service Clinic at Floriana; For Gozo: National Immunisation Clinic at Rabat, Gozo No appointments are needed for these clinics. The service will be available between 7.30 a.m. and 2.00 p.m. on the ollowing dates: Saturday, 7 and Sunday 8 November; Saturday, 14 and Sunday 15 November; Saturday, 21 and Sunday 22 November; Saturday, 28 and Sunday 29 November; Saturday, 5 and Sunday 6 December; Saturday, 12 and Sunday, 13 December; Sunday, 27, Monday, 28, Tuesday, 29 and Wednesday, 30 December. Parents or legal guardians accompanying the children are expected to carry with them: The ID Card for the parent or legal guardian; The booklet for the child is known as the Baby Booklet. Sessions for seasonal vaccination are also being organised this year through participating local councils, thus making the vaccination service more accessible in communities. As arrangements and dates may vary from one locality to another, the public is urged to contact their Local Council to confirm participation and get details about the date, time and location of the session. Doctors working in private practice may obtain supplies of vaccines against influenza, COVID-19 and pneumococcus purchased by the Government to administer to eligible patients in their private practice. Patients wishing to get the vaccine through their family doctor or another doctor in private practice can contact their doctor directly to verify whether this service is being offered. Influenza, COVID-19 and infections from pneumococcus can lead to serious complications, particularly among the elderly, children in the six-month to five-year group, those with chronic medical conditions, pregnant women, health service workers and other vulnerable people. The current monitoring in Malta indicates an increase in illnesses with symptoms similar to influenza as soon as the respiratory infection season starts, although levels generally remain similar to those registered in the same period in previous seasons. No increase related to hospital admissions has been registered so far. The epidemiologic situation will continue to be monitored during autumn and winter. The health authorities say the public should not put off getting vaccinated until respiratory infections spread. Vaccination before the increase in circulation of these infections in winter allows the body to develop protection and helps to reduce the risk of serious illness. Protect yourself and those around you – get vaccinated this winter. Less influenza, more time for yourself! Visit the influenza.gov.mt website for more information. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Early Flu Season Is Here! Hospitalizations Hit a 16-Year High - The People's Pharmacy

News Brief
The early flu season in the United States is dominated by H1N1pdm09, with hospitalizations reaching a 16-year high. As of October 17, 4,811 laboratory-confirmed influenza admissions were reported, with the highest rate among people 65 and older. While the situation is concerning, the vaccine effectiveness remains uncertain, and ongoing monitoring is in place.
Original source text (English)
Go Ad-Free Cart0Login The People's Perspective on Medicine Drug Information Home Remedies Health Concerns Libraries Podcasts Health eGuides Store Early Flu Season Is Here! Hospitalizations Hit a 16-Year High Hospitalizations are rising in this early flu season as H1N1 dominates. What can Australia and South Korea teach us? What can you do? 4.4 - 16 ratings Influenza (flu) Joe Graedon October 29, 2026 1 Comment Joe Graedon - October 29, 2026 - 1 Comment Influenza does not usually command much attention before Halloween. Many people don’t contemplate a flu shot until a week or two before Thanksgiving. Then they get worried about traveling to family get-togethers. This year is different, though. An early flu season is already showing up in laboratory tests, emergency rooms and hospital admissions. Although influenza activity remains relatively low nationwide, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention is reporting that flu activity has been climbing since late August or early September. The strain hitting early and hard is A(H1N1), which is quite different from the influenza A(H3N2/subclade-K) that affected Australia and New Zealand during the 2026 winter in the Southern Hemisphere. Those countries experienced a relatively subdued flu season. Influenza got off to a slow start down under, and overall activity remained substantially lower than in 2025.. Our Early Warning System Failed Us We frequently look to Australia and New Zealand for an early warning about what we should expect six months later in the Northern Hemisphere. It turns out that we should have been studying South Korea instead. It was the canary in the coal mine. Scientists there were reporting H1N1pdm09 by August and early September. Hospitalizations climbed rapidly. Guess what is hitting the United States. You got it: H1N1pdm09. And just as in South Korea, hospitalizations are climbing. During the week ending October 17, the CDC’s influenza hospitalization surveillance system recorded an unusually high hospitalization rate for this early in the season, somewhat reminiscent of what was observed in South Korea. No one is panicking, but it is the highest hospitalization rate for this point in the flu season since 2010-2011. An Early Flu Season Is Showing Up in Several Ways The hospitalization numbers are not the only warning sign. The percentage of specimens testing positive for influenza has been increasing. So have emergency department visits involving flu and laboratory-confirmed influenza hospitalizations. During the week ending October 17, hospitals participating in the CDC’s National Healthcare Safety Network reported 4,811 laboratory-confirmed influenza admissions. The hospitalization rate was highest among people 65 and older. There is also evidence that influenza is getting an unusually early start in certain parts of the country. Louisiana and Hawaii had reached “moderate” levels of influenza-like illness activity while most states were still categorized as low or minimal. Walgreens has reported another intriguing signal. Based on testing, antiviral prescriptions and sales of cold and flu products, the pharmacy chain estimated that flu activity was running roughly four weeks ahead of recent flu seasons. That doesn’t mean we are headed for a disastrous winter. It does mean that influenza may not be waiting until December or January to make its presence felt. New York Flu Cases Jump Sharply New York provides a dramatic example. More than 3,500 laboratory-confirmed flu cases were reported during the week ending October 17. That was roughly a 400 percent increase compared with the corresponding week last year, according to state health data. A percentage increase like that can sound scarier than it really is when starting numbers are small. Nevertheless, combined with the national hospitalization data, it reinforces the message that influenza is appearing earlier than usual this fall. H1N1 Dominates This Early Flu Season So far, this is overwhelmingly an influenza A season. Of 323 influenza viruses reported by public health laboratories during the week ending October 17, 318 were influenza A and only five were influenza B. Even more striking, among the influenza A viruses that were subtyped, 239 of 241, or 99.2 percent, were influenza A(H1N1)pdm09. None were ordinary seasonal H3N2. The H1N1 viruses circulating now are descendants of the virus that emerged during the 2009 pandemic. They have been circulating as seasonal influenza viruses ever since. There is a wrinkle. The CDC reports that recently circulating H1N1 viruses belong predominantly to a group called D.3.1.1 and are continuing to undergo genetic changes. Does that mean this year’s flu vaccine will be a poor match? At this point, nobody knows. The CDC states: “It is too early to know what viruses may predominate or to say whether there might be implications on flu vaccine effectiveness this season.” That uncertainty is worth keeping in mind. Influenza viruses are moving targets, and vaccine effectiveness varies from year to year. This year’s shots may be good...but they are never perfect. We have been tracking effectiveness since 2004. Here is a link to our analysis. Over that time period, the influenza vaccine effectiveness has averaged 40%. Some may think that’s pretty good. I would have hoped for something a lot better. Is Now the Time to Get a Flu Shot? For people who plan to get vaccinated against influenza this year, the clock is ticking. The CDC recommends annual influenza vaccination for nearly everyone six months of age and older. For most people, September or October is considered an ideal time. There is also a practical reason not to wait much longer. It generally takes about two weeks after vaccination for the immune response to develop. A vaccination around Halloween should therefore provide time for an immune response before Thanksgiving travel and family gatherings. People 65 and older deserve particular attention. They currently have the highest influenza hospitalization rate, and the CDC preferentially recommends one of three vaccines for this age group: a high-dose vaccine, an adjuvanted vaccine or a recombinant vaccine. Vaccination cannot guarantee that someone won’t catch influenza. The CDC itself acknowledges that vaccinated people can still become infected and transmit flu. A much bigger question involves whether a flu shot reduces the severity of an infection if someone catches influenza. Some observational studies have found lower rates of intensive-care admission, organ failure or death among vaccinated people who nevertheless develop laboratory-confirmed influenza. Other studies, however, have found little or no reduction in severity. Because these comparisons are observational rather than randomized, they cannot completely rule out differences between vaccinated and unvaccinated patients that might influence the outcome. Bottom line: Flu shots moderately reduce the likelihood of catching the flu and might reduce the intensity of the infection. No guarantees, though. “I Thought It Was Just a Regular Cold” One recent story illustrates why influenza should not always be dismissed as “just the flu.” ABC7 New York reported this week on 63-year-old Frank Bosco of East Northport, New York. During the previous flu season, what he initially thought was an ordinary cold turned into a catastrophic influenza infection. Bosco spent 116 days in three hospitals. At one point he required dialysis and was placed on a lung-transplant list. He and his wife told ABC that neither had previously gotten flu vaccinations. They are now urging other people to take influenza more seriously. One person’s experience cannot tell us how well vaccination will work for everyone. But his story is a reminder that influenza can occasionally cause devastating complications even when an illness initially seems routine. Don’t Forget Antiviral Treatment Vaccination isn’t the only tool in the toolbox when it comes to fighting off influenza. Prescription antiviral medicines such as oseltamivir (Tamiflu) and baloxavir (Xofluza) can be useful, especially for people at high risk of complications. These drugs generally work best when started as early as possible after symptoms begin. They are not silver bullets, however. Such drugs have a modest impact on the duration of a flu attack. But we do have a caveat here. Some folks report impressive results while others say the antivirals were worthless or caused unpleasant side effects. We cannot account for the huge variability in responses we have seen from visitors to this website. Here are just a few of our articles: Protecting Yourself from the Flu with Tamiflu What Should You Know About Vaccine Effectiveness? Does Tamiflu Speed Recovery from Influenza? What about Side Effects? People who develop trouble breathing, chest pain, confusion, severe dehydration or symptoms that improve and then suddenly worsen should seek immediate medical attention. The Bottom Line on the Early Flu Season No one can predict in October how severe the 2026-2027 influenza season will ultimately become. The CDC emphasizes that national activity is still relatively low. But this year’s early flu season is providing signals worth paying attention to. Positive tests are rising, emergency room visits are increasing and flu hospitalizations are occurring earlier than usual. H1N1 is currently dominating the viral landscape. For people who intend to get vaccinated, waiting until influenza is widespread may not make much sense. With Thanksgiving less than a month away, now may be an especially reasonable time to discuss flu vaccination with a health professional. And if influenza symptoms strike, especially in someone at high risk for complications, please ask promptly whether antiviral treatment would be appropriate. Because you can now purchase home influenza test kits at many pharmacies, why not have a box on hand in case you start to sniffle and sneeze?. 流感病例上升:何时在家休息、何时去看医生 - 每日邮报

Flu Cases on the Rise: When to Rest at Home and When to See a Doctor - JurnalPost
新闻简报正如印度尼西亚卫生部在一份新闻声明中指出的那样,该文章报告了 2026 年 9 月印度尼西亚流感活动有所增加。哨兵 监测 系统在 265 个样本中发现了 148 例流感阳性病例,阳性率为 56%,但这并不等于 56% 的人口被感染。最常见的病毒是流感 甲型H1N1流感病毒pdm09,而流感 甲型H3N2流感病毒 K 亚支迄今为止已被识别出 22 次。文章警告说,流感可导致严重并发症,尤其是幼儿、老年人和慢性病患者等高危人群。它还强调了区分流感与其他呼吸道感染的重要性,以及在出现症状的前 48 小时内使用 抗病毒药物 治疗 的必要性。卫生部随后指出,截至 2026 年 10 月 24 日的最新每周数据中,阳性率下降至 32%,强调需要仔细解读数据。

2026 年 10 月 31 日 18:54 WITA;2026 年 9 月 28 日;2026 年 10 月 24 日。
地点与对象
印度尼西亚;普通人群,重点关注幼儿、老人、孕妇、慢性病患者、免疫系统受损等高危人群。
事件
2026 年 9 月,印度尼西亚的流感病例有所增加,卫生部报告流感活动加剧。本文讨论了识别流感症状的重要性以及特定病例中医疗护理的必要性。
规模、影响与风险
2026年第38周,流感活动增加,通过前哨监测系统检测的265份标本中,148份确诊为阳性,阳性率为56%。然而,这个比率并不反映感染人口的百分比。最常检测到的病毒是流感 甲型H1N1流感病毒pdm09,流感 甲型H3N2流感病毒 K 亚型截至该时期已检测到 22 次。文章强调,流感可导致肺炎等并发症,尤其是在高危人群中。

卫生部于2026年9月28日发布新闻声明,并于2026年10月24日发布监测报告,表明阳性率下降至32%。该文章强调了根据观察期解释数据的重要性,并就何时寻求医疗帮助以及何时在家控制症状提供了指导。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Flu Cases on the Rise: When to Rest at Home and When to See a Doctor - JurnalPost

News Brief
The article reports an increase in influenza activity in Indonesia during September 2026, as noted by the Ministry of Health in a press statement. The sentinel surveillance system found 148 positive influenza cases out of 265 specimens, resulting in a 56% positivity rate, though this does not equate to 56% of the population being infected. The most common virus detected was influenza A(H1N1)pdm09, while influenza A(H3N2) subclade K was identified 22 times up to that point. The article warns that influenza can lead to severe complications, especially in high-risk groups such as young children, the elderly, and those with chronic diseases. It also highlights the importance of distinguishing influenza from other respiratory infections and the need for antiviral treatment within the first 48 hours of symptoms. The Ministry of Health later noted a decrease in the positivity rate to 32% in the latest weekly data, as of October 24, 2026, emphasizing the need for careful data interpretation.
Original source text (English)
Flu Cases on the Rise: When to Rest at Home and When to See a Doctor Authors: Ainur Rosyida, Najwa Kholishah, Farrel Oktavian E. R, Mohammad Yusron Nafis, Seilya Sapna Bella Editor: Shella Paramitha October 31, 2026, 18:54 WITA Choose Save as PDF in the print dialog. An illustration of a healthcare worker checking a patient's body temperature. The public needs to recognize influenza symptoms and warning signs that require medical attention. (Photo: Antoni Shkraba/Pexels) JurnalPost.com - Complaints of coughs, colds, fevers, and sore throats have become a public concern as influenza activity increased in Indonesia in September 2026. Although symptoms are often dismissed as minor illnesses, influenza still requires vigilance, especially among groups at risk of complications. In a press statement on 28 September 2026, the Ministry of Health (Kemenkes) reported that influenza activity increased in the 38 week of 2026. Out of 265 specimens examined through the sentinel surveillance system, 148 were confirmed positive for influenza. A positivity rate of 56 percent might sound alarming. However, it is important to understand that this percentage does not mean 56 percent of the Indonesian population is currently infected with influenza. So, how should the public respond to the rise in influenza activity? When can symptoms be managed with rest at home, and when does a patient need immediate medical help? Understanding Influenza Positivity Rates Accurately The 56 percent figure represents the proportion of positive results from specimens of patients examined through the sentinel surveillance system. This system uses selected health facilities to monitor disease progression and viral spread. Therefore, this figure cannot be equated to the percentage of the Indonesian population currently ill. The Ministry of Health explained that the increase in influenza activity in September still follows a common seasonal pattern. During this period, the most frequently detected virus was influenza A(H1N1)pdm09. For comparison, Ministry of Health surveillance data from 2026 through the 38 week showed 1,527 positive influenza specimens out of a total of 8,757 tests, or approximately 17 percent. Meanwhile, influenza A(H3N2) subclade K, often associated with the term superflu , was detected 22 times throughout 2026 up to that period. This figure is lower than the 166 findings in 2025. The term superflu is not an official term used by the World Health Organization (WHO) or health authorities to designate a new type of influenza. Influenza trends continue to change. In a surveillance report published on 24 October 2026, the proportion of positive influenza specimens in the latest weekly data was recorded as having decreased to 32 percent from 45 percent the previous week. This highlights the importance of interpreting data based on the observation period rather than viewing a single number as a reflection of an ongoing condition. Influenza Is More Than Just a Common Cough and Cold Influenza is a respiratory tract infection caused by influenza viruses, primarily types A and B. The virus spreads through respiratory particles expelled when an infected person coughs, sneezes, talks, or breathes. The risk of transmission increases when someone is in close proximity to an infected person, especially in enclosed spaces with poor ventilation. Influenza symptoms generally appear suddenly. Patients may experience fever, cough, runny nose, sore throat, headache, muscle aches, and significant fatigue. These symptoms may superficially resemble the common cold or common cold . However, influenza tends to cause more severe complaints and can trigger specific complications. Therefore, not every cough and cold referred to as the flu in daily conversation is caused by the influenza virus. Other respiratory viral infections can also cause similar symptoms. Most influenza patients recover within about one week. However, young children, the elderly, pregnant women, people with chronic diseases, and those with compromised immune systems are at a higher risk of experiencing complications, including pneumonia or lung infections. Antibiotics Are Not the Cure for Influenza One common misconception still found in the community is the belief that antibiotics can be used to treat all types of coughs, colds, and fevers. In reality, antibiotics work against infections caused by bacteria, whereas influenza is a disease caused by a viral infection. Taking antibiotics without a medical indication does not make influenza heal faster. Inappropriate use of antibiotics can also increase the risk of antimicrobial resistance. Antibiotics are only necessary if a doctor identifies a secondary bacterial infection or specific complications that require such treatment. In addition to antibiotics, the public also needs to understand the use of antiviral drugs like oseltamivir. Antiviral drugs do not need to be given to every influenza patient. Doctors will consider their use based on the patient's condition, the severity of the disease, and the risk of complications. Antiviral treatment generally provides the most optimal benefits if started within the first 48 hours after symptoms appear. However, for patients with severe influenza or those in high-risk groups, treatment can still be beneficial even if started after that period. Therefore, the public should not purchase or consume antiviral drugs without examination and guidance from medical professionals. When Is It Enough to Manage the Flu with Rest at Home? For patients with mild symptoms, a generally stable physical condition, and no risk factors for complications, influenza can usually be managed through self-care at home. Simple steps include getting enough rest, increasing water intake, eating a balanced, nutritious diet, and monitoring symptom progression. Fever reducers or pain relievers can be used according to the dosage instructions and the patient's health condition. However, it must be understood that these medications are intended to relieve symptoms, not to kill the influenza virus. For children, medication use must be adjusted according to age and the advice of health professionals. While ill, patients should reduce close contact with others to avoid transmitting the virus to family members, coworkers, or people in their surroundings. Self-care must also be accompanied by monitoring. If symptoms worsen, do not improve, or if unusual complaints arise, a medical examination is necessary. For high-risk groups, consultation with a health professional should be sought as soon as symptoms appear, even if the complaints do not yet seem severe. Recognize Danger Signs, Don't Delay Seeing a Doctor Although most influenza cases can resolve on their own, there are several danger signs that should not be ignored. Patients need to seek immediate medical examination if they experience a high fever that does not improve for more than three days, difficulty drinking, or a condition that initially improved but then worsened again. Symptoms such as shortness of breath, rapid breathing, severe or persistent chest pain, seizures, difficulty waking up, or bluish lips and face require emergency medical assistance. In children, parents need to observe whether the chest or the spaces between the ribs appear to pull inward when the child breathes. This condition may indicate respiratory distress. Signs of dehydration should also be watched for, such as very infrequent urination, dry mouth, lack of tears when crying, or an inability to drink properly. Infants under three months old who develop a fever also require immediate medical assessment. Severe symptoms should not be ignored just because the patient has not yet had a fever for three days. Examinations must be performed according to the severity of the condition experienced. Flu Prevention Starts with Simple Habits Preventing influenza transmission does not always require complex steps. Simple habits practiced consistently can help reduce the risk of infection. Washing hands with soap and running water, practicing cough and sneeze etiquette, and wearing a mask when ill are steps that can be applied in daily life. Maintaining room ventilation and reducing close contact with others when experiencing symptoms of respiratory tract infection can also help prevent transmission. Additionally, an annual influenza vaccination is a protective measure that can be considered according to the recommendations of health professionals, especially for groups at high risk of complications. The influenza vaccine does not guarantee that a person will be completely protected from infection. However, vaccination can help reduce the risk of severe illness, complications, and the need for hospitalization. The increase in seasonal influenza activity should be met with vigilance, not panic. The public needs to understand that not every cough and cold is influenza, antibiotics are not a cure for viral infections, and not every patient requires hospital care. The most important thing is to recognize your body's condition, perform appropriate self-care, and know when to seek medical help. With accurate information and consistent preventive habits, the public can protect themselves while helping to reduce the risk of transmitting influenza to others. 新闻简报CDC 报告称,截至 2026 年 10 月 24 日当周,流感入院人数大幅增加,实验室确诊病例达到 6,100 例,较前一周增加 27%。这是本季首例儿童死于流感,但儿童的年龄、州和 疫苗接种 状态并未透露。疾病预防控制中心将死亡与 H1N1 病毒株联系起来,并指出 4 岁以下儿童和 5 至 17 岁儿童因流感就诊的急诊人数有所增加。尽管如此,疾病预防控制中心表示,季节性流感活动在全国范围内较低,但在许多地区有所增加。该机构没有提供有关孩子情况的进一步细节,强调需要继续监测和提高公共卫生意识。这种情况凸显了“疫苗接种”和为弱势群体(特别是幼儿和老年人)提供及时医疗服务的重要性。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Flu Hospital Admissions Jump 27% in One Week to 6,1200 as CDC Records First Child Death This Season

News Brief
The CDC reported a significant rise in flu hospital admissions, with 6,100 lab-confirmed cases in the week ending Oct. 24, 2026, marking a 27% increase from the previous week. This is the first child death from the flu this season, though the child's age, state, and vaccination status were not disclosed. The CDC linked the death to the H1N1 strain and noted that emergency department visits for flu increased among children up to age 4 and those aged 5 to 17. Despite this, the CDC described seasonal flu activity as low nationally but increasing in many regions. The agency did not provide further details on the child's circumstances, highlighting the need for continued monitoring and public health awareness. The situation underscores the importance of vaccination and timely medical care for vulnerable populations, particularly young children and older adults.

Conditions Flu Hospital Admissions Jump 27% in One Week to 6,1200 as CDC Records First Child Death This Season Published Nov 01, 2026 8:00 AM EST By Joseph James ADEK BERRY | AFP via Getty Images Share on Twitter Share on Facebook Share on WhatsApp U.S. hospitals reported 6,100 lab-confirmed flu admissions in the week ending Oct. 24, a 27% jump from 4,811 the week before, and the CDC received its first report of a child dying from flu this season. The death occurred in the week ending Oct. 10 and was linked to the H1N1 strain, according to the agency's Week 42 FluView report. The CDC did not release the child's age, state, or vaccination status. For parents, the shift is visible beyond hospital beds. The share of emergency department visits for flu rose for children up to age 4, and for kids 5 to 17, while holding steady for adults 65 and older, Becker's Hospital Review reported. That puts households with young children and older relatives in the position of making practical decisions about shots, symptoms, and when to seek care. The CDC still describes seasonal flu activity as low nationally but increasing in many parts of the country. It also cautioned that "an earlier increase in activity does not necessarily lead to a higher seasonal peak or greater overall severity." Since MedicalDaily's Week 41 Report MedicalDaily previously reported that the early flu season pushed hospitalization rates to a mid-October high not seen since 2010–11, based on the week ending Oct. 17. At that time, the national admission rate was 1.4 per 100,000 people, test positivity was 5.7%, and no pediatric flu deaths had been reported for the 2026-27 season. One week later, the main measures moved up. The admission rate climbed to 1.8 per 100,000. Lab positivity rose to 7.6%, with 4,061 of 53,102 specimens testing positive. Flu accounted for 1.2% of emergency visits, up from 0.9%, and flu-like illness reached 2.4% of outpatient visits, still below the national baseline of 2.9%. The CDC's FluSurv-NET network, which tracks hospitalizations in selected areas, estimated the weekly rate at 1.9 to 2.5 per 100,000 once delayed reports arrive. Becker's noted that this is the highest rate for that week of the season since 2010–11. The CDC estimates the season has caused at least 1 million illnesses, 16,000 hospitalizations, and 900 deaths so far. The geography has also shifted. The highest regional admission rate is now in HHS Region 9, which covers California, Arizona, Nevada, and Hawaii, at 2.5 per 100,000. Region 10 in the Northwest was the only area above its baseline for outpatient flu-like illness. Nine metro areas reached high activity, up from one the week before. Children and Adults 65 and Older Carry the Heaviest Load Risk is not evenly spread. Adults 65 and older had the highest national admission rate at 5.8 per 100,000, followed by children 4 and younger at 1.8 and adults 50 to 64 at 1.6. In FluSurv-NET, the cumulative rate for adults 65 and older reached 13.3 per 100,000, more than three times the rate for any younger age group. Among racial and ethnic groups, non-Hispanic Black residents had the highest age-adjusted hospitalization rate in FluSurv-NET. The CDC also counted one more pediatric death from last season, an influenza B case, bringing the 2025-26 total to 199 children. Nearly all subtyped influenza A viruses this week were H1N1, at 98.9%. The CDC said it is seeing more genetic diversity among circulating H1N1 viruses than it has seen before. It is too early to know whether that will affect vaccine effectiveness, though 94.2% of tested H1N1 viruses were well recognized by antibodies to this year's vaccine strain. Andrew Pekosz, a virologist at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, described the moment as early but not settled. "Cases are still relatively low, so we're still in the early phases of a flu surge," he told TODAY. On vaccine match, he added, "We may have less immunity, not no immunity." Household Decisions for Parents and Caregivers The CDC recommends a yearly flu shot for everyone 6 months and older, and it is not too late. Protection takes about two weeks to build, so families planning holiday gatherings with infants or older relatives may want to schedule shots now. Antiviral medicines work best when started within two days of getting sick. They matter most for children under 5, adults 65 and older, pregnant people, and people with asthma, heart disease, diabetes, or weakened immune systems. CDC testing found no resistance to baloxavir, and only 1.5% of tested H1N1 viruses showed reduced response to oseltamivir, sold as Tamiflu. Flu usually comes on suddenly with fever, cough, sore throat, body aches, and fatigue. Children who have trouble breathing, bluish lips, signs of dehydration, a fever above 104 degrees that does not come down with fever-reducing medicine, or who are not alert need emergency care. Adults with chest pain, confusion, or symptoms that improve and then return worse should also be seen urgently. Keeping sick children home helps protect classmates and vulnerable family members. Next FluView and the Forecast Ahead The CDC's FluSight forecasts project that flu admissions will likely keep rising nationally, with a range of 4,800 to 23,200 new weekly admissions for the week ending Nov. 14. That wide range reflects real uncertainty about how fast the season will build. The next weekly FluView update is expected next week. It will show whether admissions keep climbing, whether Region 9 continues to lead, and whether more pediatric deaths are reported. MedicalDaily will track those figures and state-level reports as the season develops. The newest numbers show flu spreading earlier than usual, with the first child death of the season confirmed. The season's severity is still unknown, but a flu shot now and prompt contact with a clinician for high-risk family members remain the most useful steps. Admissions rose 27% to 6,100 in the week ending Oct. 24, up from 4,811 the week before. Has a child died from flu this season? Yes. The CDC received its first pediatric flu death report of 2026-27. The death occurred in the week ending Oct. 10 and was linked to H1N1. Who has the highest hospitalization rate? Adults 65 and older, at 5.8 per 100,000, followed by children 4 and younger. Where is flu activity highest? HHS Region 9, covering California, Arizona, Nevada, and Hawaii, had the highest admission rate. Does an early start mean a severe season? Not necessarily. The CDC says an earlier rise does not necessarily lead to a higher peak or greater severity. Is it too late for a flu shot? No. The CDC recommends a yearly shot for everyone 6 months and older. Protection builds in about two weeks. View five news elements
Time
The event date is the week ending Oct. 24, 2026, with the publication date being Nov.
Location and population
The event occurred in the United States, with a focus on children up to age 4 and those aged 5 to 17, as well as adults 65 and older.
Event
Flu hospital admissions increased by 27% in one week to 6,100, and the CDC recorded its first child death from the flu this season.
Scale, impact and risk
There were 6,100 lab-confirmed flu admissions in the week ending Oct. 24, 2026, marking a 27% increase from the previous week. The CDC received its first report of a child death from flu this season, though the child's age, state, and vaccination status were not disclosed. The CDC also noted an increase in emergency department visits for flu among children up to age 4 and those aged 5 to 17.
Response, status and uncertainty
The CDC described seasonal flu activity as low nationally but increasing in many parts of the country. The child's death was linked to the H1N1 strain, but no further details were provided. The CDC did not release the child's age, state, or vaccination status.
